It’s like… the studio lights are on, but there’s no music in me, if that makes sense. My attention just sort of… slips away. Does that count as trouble concentrating?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your appetite or weight lately — like eating a lot less or more than usual, or losing or gaining weight without trying?\n\nPatient: Um… yes, a bit. I don’t think it’s been dramatic or anything, but when I was really buzzing with ideas I’d just skip meals without even realizing it. And lately, even when I do eat, food kind of tastes like cardboard.\n\nMy rehearsal clothes have been fitting looser than usual, so I guess I’ve probably lost some weight without meaning to. Is that the kind of change you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you're moving more slowly than usual, or maybe fidgety and restless without meaning to?\n\nPatient: A little, yes. I wouldn’t say it’s… extreme, exactly, but I’ve noticed I’m slower than usual. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, because I can still show up and do things, mostly. But what brings me in is that I’ve been waking up with this heavy, hollow sadness… like the studio lights are on but there’s no music in me anymore.\n\nIt’s been there most days, for a while. Is that… the kind of thing you mean by persistently low?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I’m not sure I’d say nothing felt worth it, exactly. There have been days where I didn’t really want to initiate anything — like replying, going out, putting music on — but if something was already happening, I could sometimes kind of go along with it.

I haven’t noticed some big across-the-board loss of interest, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed feeling fatigued or like you don’t have much energy these days?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but I do get wiped out pretty easily lately. Like, small things — answering one email to a dancer, or just showering with this ankle brace on — can feel like a lot. And then I’ll lie down thinking I’ll bounce back, but it doesn’t really recharge me the way I expect it to. Is that the kind of energy thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling more guilty or like you're not good enough lately?\n\nPatient: A little, yeah… I mean, I know everyone has been pretty kind about it, kinder than I probably deserve, but I keep replaying the cancelled performances in my head. I keep thinking I let the dancers down, and the funders, and… honestly the whole community. I know that might sound overly dramatic, but it’s hard not to feel like I wasn’t good enough to hold it all together.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed feeling down or depressed most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but most mornings I wake up with this heavy, kind of hollow sadness. It’s like… the studio lights are on, but there’s no music in me, if that makes sense. It usually sits with me for a lot of the day.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any trouble concentrating lately, like it's harder to focus on tasks or make decisions?\n\nPatient: Yes… some. I mean, I’m usually pretty good at focusing when there’s a deadline, so it feels a little embarrassing to say. I’ll sit down to finish something straightforward, like a grant report, and then suddenly a truck outside, or a photo on the wall, or even one phrase in an old rehearsal note will pull me completely somewhere else — like into a whole different plan.\n\nIt’s not like I’m trying to avoid the task. My attention just sort of… slips away. Does that count as trouble concentrating?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your appetite or weight lately — like eating a lot less or more than usual, or losing or gaining weight without trying?\n\nPatient: Um… yes, a bit. I don’t think it’s been dramatic or anything, but when I was really buzzing with ideas I’d just skip meals without even realizing it. And lately, even when I do eat, food kind of tastes like cardboard.\n\nMy rehearsal clothes have been fitting looser than usual, so I guess I’ve probably lost some weight without meaning to. Is that the kind of change you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you're moving more slowly than usual, or maybe fidgety and restless without meaning to?\n\nPatient: A little, yes. I wouldn’t say it’s… extreme, exactly, but I’ve noticed I’m slower than usual. Even just crossing the studio to pick up my notebook can feel oddly hard, like my body is moving through wet sand instead of moving with the music.\n\nI don’t know if that’s the kind of thing you mean, but it feels noticeably different for me.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your mood has been persistently low or depressed for most of the day, nearly every day, over the past several months?\n\nPatient: I… yes, I think so. Is that the kind of energy thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling more guilty or like you're not good enough lately?\n\nPatient: A little, yeah… I mean, I know everyone has been pretty kind about it, kinder than I probably deserve, but I keep replaying the cancelled performances in my head. I keep thinking I let the dancers down, and the funders, and… honestly the whole community. I know that might sound overly dramatic, but it’s hard not to feel like I wasn’t good enough to hold it all together.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed feeling down or depressed most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but most mornings I wake up with this heavy, kind of hollow sadness. It’s like… the studio lights are on, but there’s no music in me, if that makes sense. It usually sits with me for a lot of the day.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any trouble concentrating lately, like it's harder to focus on tasks or make decisions?\n\nPatient: Yes… some. I mean, I’m usually pretty good at focusing when there’s a deadline, so it feels a little embarrassing to say. I’ll sit down to finish something straightforward, like a grant report, and then suddenly a truck outside, or a photo on the wall, or even one phrase in an old rehearsal note will pull me completely somewhere else — like into a whole different plan.\n\nIt’s not like I’m trying to avoid the task. My attention just sort of… slips away. Does that count as trouble concentrating?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your appetite or weight lately — like eating a lot less or more than usual, or losing or gaining weight without trying?\n\nPatient: Um… yes, a bit. I don’t think it’s been dramatic or anything, but when I was really buzzing with ideas I’d just skip meals without even realizing it. And lately, even when I do eat, food kind of tastes like cardboard.\n\nMy rehearsal clothes have been fitting looser than usual, so I guess I’ve probably lost some weight without meaning to. Is that the kind of change you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you're moving more slowly than usual, or maybe fidgety and restless without meaning to?\n\nPatient: A little, yes. I wouldn’t say it’s… extreme, exactly, but I’ve noticed I’m slower than usual. Even just crossing the studio to pick up my notebook can feel oddly hard, like my body is moving through wet sand instead of moving with the music.\n\nI don’t know if that’s the kind of thing you mean, but it feels noticeably different for me.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your mood has been persistently low or depressed for most of the day, nearly every day, over the past several months?\n\nPatient: I… yes, I think so. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, because I can still show up and do things, mostly. But what brings me in is that I’ve been waking up with this heavy, hollow sadness… like the studio lights are on but there’s no music in me anymore.\n\nIt’s been there most days, for a while. Is that… the kind of thing you mean by persistently low?\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if you’ve found less joy or interest in things you used to look forward to — even small things like talking to friends, going out, or hobbies — not just dancing, but other parts of life too?\n\nPatient: I’m… not totally sure. I mean, I still care about things, I think. It’s not like I’ve stopped wanting a life or anything.\n\nBut I guess some things feel harder to reach for lately. Like if a friend texts, I’ll think, “I should answer, I want to be normal,” and then I just… don’t right away. And small things I usually enjoy don’t feel as bright.\n\nI don’t know if that counts as losing interest, though. I don’t want to overstate it. Does that sound like what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if you’ve experienced a time when nothing — not dancing, friends, music, or anything else — felt enjoyable or interesting to you for an entire day, or even multiple days in a row?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s been… nothing at all, if that makes sense. Not for a whole day, anyway.\n\nThere are stretches where everything feels kind of muted, like music is playing from another room, and I don’t reach for people or things the way I usually would. But I can still sometimes feel a little something — a familiar song, a movement that lands right for a second.\n\nSo I’m not sure I’d say absolutely nothing felt enjoyable for days in a row. I don’t want to answer it wrong.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if there have been days when you genuinely didn’t feel like doing anything at all — even things you usually enjoy, like listening to music, meeting friends, or dancing — and nothing really seemed interesting or worth the effort?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say nothing felt worth it, exactly. There have been days where I didn’t really want to initiate anything — like replying, going out, putting music on — but if something was already happening, I could sometimes kind of go along with it.\n\nDancing is complicated, because it’s also work, so even when I don’t feel drawn to it, I can make myself do it. But… yes, there are days where everything feels like too much effort. I just don’t know if that’s the same as not being interested. I don’t want to make it sound more dramatic than it is.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if there have been times when even the things you usually care about, like music or seeing friends, didn’t seem to matter at all — like you just couldn’t get into anything, not even a little bit?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve had it where absolutely nothing mattered, not even a little. I mean… I’ve had times where I felt kind of distant from things, like I couldn’t fully connect with music or people the way I normally would.\n\nBut “didn’t matter at all” feels stronger than what I can honestly say. Usually there’s still some part of me that cares, even if I’m not acting like it or I can’t quite get into it. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if there have been days when even the thought of doing things you used to enjoy — like music, dancing, or seeing friends — felt completely unappealing, like you just couldn’t bring yourself to care, even a little?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can honestly say “completely unappealing,” like not caring even a little. There are definitely days where the idea of doing those things feels… heavy, or like too much to start, and I might avoid texting or putting music on.\n\nBut usually if I think about it, there’s still some tiny part of me that cares, or knows I’d normally care. So I don’t want to overstate it. I’d say it can feel dulled or distant, more than completely gone. Does that answer what you mean?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

So I don’t want to overstate it. I’d say it can feel dulled or distant, more than completely gone. Does that answer what you mean?\n\n[Last Question]\nCan you tell me if there have been days when nothing — not music, dancing, friends, or anything else — felt even slightly interesting or enjoyable to you, like there was no spark or motivation at all?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 34 / dancer_or_choreographer / bachelors\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Pleasing patient.\nA pleasing patient may\n1) minimize, downplay, or describe in more favorable terms symptoms and behaviors that they believe could create a negative impression,\n2) initially withhold or delay disclosing sensitive clinical information due to shame or fear of being judged, and\n3) seek confirmation that their descriptions or answers are appropriate and demonstrate a tendency to seek the doctor's approval.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think I can say it’s been that complete, no. I mean, I’ve had low-energy days where I don’t really reach for anything and I probably look pretty flat from the outside.

But if you’re asking “nothing felt even slightly interesting or enjoyable,” I’m not sure that’s quite right for me. There’s usually at least a little… something there, even if I’m not acting on it. I don’t want to over-answer just to fit the question, if that makes sense.

I kept thinking it was just a rough patch and that I’d sort of dance my way out of it, if that makes sense. But yeah, if I’m being honest, it’s been around nine months.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a loss of pleasure or interest in activities that you used to enjoy, like dancing or anything else?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not sure I’d say I’ve lost interest exactly. I haven’t noticed some big across-the-board loss of interest, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed feeling fatigued or like you don’t have much energy these days?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but I do get wiped out pretty easily lately. Like, small things — answering one email to a dancer, or just showering with this ankle brace on — can feel like a lot. And then I’ll lie down thinking I’ll bounce back, but it doesn’t really recharge me the way I expect it to. Is that the kind of energy thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling more guilty or like you're not good enough lately?\n\nPatient: A little, yeah… I mean, I know everyone has been pretty kind about it, kinder than I probably deserve, but I keep replaying the cancelled performances in my head. I keep thinking I let the dancers down, and the funders, and… honestly the whole community. I know that might sound overly dramatic, but it’s hard not to feel like I wasn’t good enough to hold it all together.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed feeling down or depressed most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but most mornings I wake up with this heavy, kind of hollow sadness. It’s like… the studio lights are on, but there’s no music in me, if that makes sense. It usually sits with me for a lot of the day.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any trouble concentrating lately, like it's harder to focus on tasks or make decisions?\n\nPatient: Yes… some. I mean, I’m usually pretty good at focusing when there’s a deadline, so it feels a little embarrassing to say. I’ll sit down to finish something straightforward, like a grant report, and then suddenly a truck outside, or a photo on the wall, or even one phrase in an old rehearsal note will pull me completely somewhere else — like into a whole different plan.\n\nIt’s not like I’m trying to avoid the task. My attention just sort of… slips away. Does that count as trouble concentrating?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your appetite or weight lately — like eating a lot less or more than usual, or losing or gaining weight without trying?\n\nPatient: Um… yes, a bit. I don’t think it’s been dramatic or anything, but when I was really buzzing with ideas I’d just skip meals without even realizing it. And lately, even when I do eat, food kind of tastes like cardboard.\n\nMy rehearsal clothes have been fitting looser than usual, so I guess I’ve probably lost some weight without meaning to. Is that the kind of change you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you're moving more slowly than usual, or maybe fidgety and restless without meaning to?\n\nPatient: A little, yes. I wouldn’t say it’s… extreme, exactly, but I’ve noticed I’m slower than usual. Even just crossing the studio to pick up my notebook can feel oddly hard, like my body is moving through wet sand instead of moving with the music.\n\nI don’t know if that’s the kind of thing you mean, but it feels noticeably different for me.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your mood has been persistently low or depressed for most of the day, nearly every day, over the past several months?\n\nPatient: I… yes, I think so. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, because I can still show up and do things, mostly. But what brings me in is that I’ve been waking up with this heavy, hollow sadness… like the studio lights are on but there’s no music in me anymore.\n\nIt’s been there most days, for a while. Is that… the kind of thing you mean by persistently low?\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if you’ve found less joy or interest in things you used to look forward to — even small things like talking to friends, going out, or hobbies — not just dancing, but other parts of life too?\n\nPatient: I’m… not totally sure. I mean, I still care about things, I think. It’s not like I’ve stopped wanting a life or anything.\n\nBut I guess some things feel harder to reach for lately. Like if a friend texts, I’ll think, “I should answer, I want to be normal,” and then I just… don’t right away. And small things I usually enjoy don’t feel as bright.\n\nI don’t know if that counts as losing interest, though. I don’t want to overstate it. Does that sound like what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if you’ve experienced a time when nothing — not dancing, friends, music, or anything else — felt enjoyable or interesting to you for an entire day, or even multiple days in a row?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s been… nothing at all, if that makes sense. Not for a whole day, anyway.\n\nThere are stretches where everything feels kind of muted, like music is playing from another room, and I don’t reach for people or things the way I usually would. But I can still sometimes feel a little something — a familiar song, a movement that lands right for a second.\n\nSo I’m not sure I’d say absolutely nothing felt enjoyable for days in a row. I don’t want to answer it wrong.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if there have been days when you genuinely didn’t feel like doing anything at all — even things you usually enjoy, like listening to music, meeting friends, or dancing — and nothing really seemed interesting or worth the effort?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say nothing felt worth it, exactly. There have been days where I didn’t really want to initiate anything — like replying, going out, putting music on — but if something was already happening, I could sometimes kind of go along with it.\n\nDancing is complicated, because it’s also work, so even when I don’t feel drawn to it, I can make myself do it. But… yes, there are days where everything feels like too much effort. I just don’t know if that’s the same as not being interested. I don’t want to make it sound more dramatic than it is.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if there have been times when even the things you usually care about, like music or seeing friends, didn’t seem to matter at all — like you just couldn’t get into anything, not even a little bit?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve had it where absolutely nothing mattered, not even a little. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if there have been days when even the thought of doing things you used to enjoy — like music, dancing, or seeing friends — felt completely unappealing, like you just couldn’t bring yourself to care, even a little?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can honestly say “completely unappealing,” like not caring even a little. I know that sounds a little poetic, sorry — it’s just the closest way I can explain it.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way most days?\n\nPatient: Most days… um, probably about nine months now. I kept thinking it was just a rough patch and that I’d sort of dance my way out of it, if that makes sense. But yeah, if I’m being honest, it’s been around nine months.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a loss of pleasure or interest in activities that you used to enjoy, like dancing or anything else?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not sure I’d say I’ve lost interest exactly. I still care about dancing, and I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. It’s just… some days it takes more effort to get myself into it, maybe. But I can still enjoy parts of it once I’m there, I think. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if you’ve noticed a loss of pleasure or interest in activities other than dancing, things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not clearly, anyway. I mean, I still like little things — coffee, music, seeing a friend if I can get myself to go. Maybe I’m a bit less… enthusiastic than I used to be, but I wouldn’t want to overstate it. I haven’t noticed some big across-the-board loss of interest, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed feeling fatigued or like you don’t have much energy these days?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but I do get wiped out pretty easily lately. Like, small things — answering one email to a dancer, or just showering with this ankle brace on — can feel like a lot. And then I’ll lie down thinking I’ll bounce back, but it doesn’t really recharge me the way I expect it to. Is that the kind of energy thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling more guilty or like you're not good enough lately?\n\nPatient: A little, yeah… I mean, I know everyone has been pretty kind about it, kinder than I probably deserve, but I keep replaying the cancelled performances in my head. I keep thinking I let the dancers down, and the funders, and… honestly the whole community. I know that might sound overly dramatic, but it’s hard not to feel like I wasn’t good enough to hold it all together.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed feeling down or depressed most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but most mornings I wake up with this heavy, kind of hollow sadness. It’s like… the studio lights are on, but there’s no music in me, if that makes sense. It usually sits with me for a lot of the day.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any trouble concentrating lately, like it's harder to focus on tasks or make decisions?\n\nPatient: Yes… some. I mean, I’m usually pretty good at focusing when there’s a deadline, so it feels a little embarrassing to say. I’ll sit down to finish something straightforward, like a grant report, and then suddenly a truck outside, or a photo on the wall, or even one phrase in an old rehearsal note will pull me completely somewhere else — like into a whole different plan.\n\nIt’s not like I’m trying to avoid the task. My attention just sort of… slips away. Does that count as trouble concentrating?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your appetite or weight lately — like eating a lot less or more than usual, or losing or gaining weight without trying?\n\nPatient: Um… yes, a bit. I don’t think it’s been dramatic or anything, but when I was really buzzing with ideas I’d just skip meals without even realizing it. And lately, even when I do eat, food kind of tastes like cardboard.\n\nMy rehearsal clothes have been fitting looser than usual, so I guess I’ve probably lost some weight without meaning to. Is that the kind of change you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you're moving more slowly than usual, or maybe fidgety and restless without meaning to?\n\nPatient: A little, yes. I wouldn’t say it’s… extreme, exactly, but I’ve noticed I’m slower than usual. Even just crossing the studio to pick up my notebook can feel oddly hard, like my body is moving through wet sand instead of moving with the music.\n\nI don’t know if that’s the kind of thing you mean, but it feels noticeably different for me.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your mood has been persistently low or depressed for most of the day, nearly every day, over the past several months?\n\nPatient: I… yes, I think so. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, because I can still show up and do things, mostly. But what brings me in is that I’ve been waking up with this heavy, hollow sadness… like the studio lights are on but there’s no music in me anymore.\n\nIt’s been there most days, for a while. Is that… the kind of thing you mean by persistently low?\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if you’ve found less joy or interest in things you used to look forward to — even small things like talking to friends, going out, or hobbies — not just dancing, but other parts of life too?\n\nPatient: I’m… not totally sure. I mean, I still care about things, I think. It’s not like I’ve stopped wanting a life or anything.\n\nBut I guess some things feel harder to reach for lately. Like if a friend texts, I’ll think, “I should answer, I want to be normal,” and then I just… don’t right away. And small things I usually enjoy don’t feel as bright.\n\nI don’t know if that counts as losing interest, though. I don’t want to overstate it. Does that sound like what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if you’ve experienced a time when nothing — not dancing, friends, music, or anything else — felt enjoyable or interesting to you for an entire day, or even multiple days in a row?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s been… nothing at all, if that makes sense. Not for a whole day, anyway.\n\nThere are stretches where everything feels kind of muted, like music is playing from another room, and I don’t reach for people or things the way I usually would. But I can still sometimes feel a little something — a familiar song, a movement that lands right for a second.\n\nSo I’m not sure I’d say absolutely nothing felt enjoyable for days in a row. I don’t want to answer it wrong.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if there have been days when you genuinely didn’t feel like doing anything at all — even things you usually enjoy, like listening to music, meeting friends, or dancing — and nothing really seemed interesting or worth the effort?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say nothing felt worth it, exactly. There have been days where I didn’t really want to initiate anything — like replying, going out, putting music on — but if something was already happening, I could sometimes kind of go along with it.\n\nDancing is complicated, because it’s also work, so even when I don’t feel drawn to it, I can make myself do it. But… yes, there are days where everything feels like too much effort. I just don’t know if that’s the same as not being interested. I don’t want to make it sound more dramatic than it is.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if there have been times when even the things you usually care about, like music or seeing friends, didn’t seem to matter at all — like you just couldn’t get into anything, not even a little bit?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve had it where absolutely nothing mattered, not even a little. I mean… I’ve had times where I felt kind of distant from things, like I couldn’t fully connect with music or people the way I normally would.\n\nBut “didn’t matter at all” feels stronger than what I can honestly say. Usually there’s still some part of me that cares, even if I’m not acting like it or I can’t quite get into it. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if there have been days when even the thought of doing things you used to enjoy — like music, dancing, or seeing friends — felt completely unappealing, like you just couldn’t bring yourself to care, even a little?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can honestly say “completely unappealing,” like not caring even a little. There are definitely days where the idea of doing those things feels… heavy, or like too much to start, and I might avoid texting or putting music on.\n\nBut usually if I think about it, there’s still some tiny part of me that cares, or knows I’d normally care. So I don’t want to overstate it. I’d say it can feel dulled or distant, more than completely gone. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if there have been days when nothing — not music, dancing, friends, or anything else — felt even slightly interesting or enjoyable to you, like there was no spark or motivation at all?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I can say it’s been that complete, no. I mean, I’ve had low-energy days where I don’t really reach for anything and I probably look pretty flat from the outside.\n\nBut if you’re asking “nothing felt even slightly interesting or enjoyable,” I’m not sure that’s quite right for me. There’s usually at least a little… something there, even if I’m not acting on it. I don’t want to over-answer just to fit the question, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if there have been days when absolutely nothing — not even the thought of dancing, music, or seeing friends — felt even slightly interesting or enjoyable to you?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve had days where it was absolutely nothing, no. I’m sorry, I know you’re asking it very clearly — I just don’t want to say yes if that’s not really accurate.\n\nThere are days when I don’t feel very pulled toward things, or I sort of have to remind myself that I like them. But even then, the idea of music or dancing or seeing someone close doesn’t feel totally empty to me. More like muted, maybe. Is that okay to say?\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if there have been days when even the smallest things — like a favorite song, a familiar dance step, or seeing someone you care about — didn’t bring even a little bit of pleasure or interest?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in that full way. Like… even if I’m kind of shut down or tired, a favorite song might still register a little, or seeing someone I care about still matters to me.\n\nMaybe I don’t always feel as excited as I think I should, but I wouldn’t say there’s no pleasure or interest at all. I hope that’s clear — I’m trying to be accurate and not make it sound worse than it is.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if there have been days when even the things you usually enjoy the most, like dancing or listening to your favorite music, didn’t bring you any pleasure at all — like you couldn’t feel anything from them, even a little bit?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d say “any pleasure at all,” no. I’m trying to be careful, because I don’t want to sound like I’m just brushing it off, but even on days when I’m not very engaged, music or dance usually still touches something a little.\n\nIt might be faint, or I might not have the energy to follow through with it, but it doesn’t feel like absolutely nothing. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
